2. Course Category: Core for B.Tech (Mathematics & Computing)
3. Prerequisite Concepts: Calculus - Taylor’s series, Taylor’s remainder theorem, Con-
tinuity and Differentiability of functions of one and several variables, Gradient of a
function, Jacobian matrix, Hessian matrix of a function. Linear/Matrix Algebra - Rank
of a matrix, Column space & Nullspace, Eigenvalues and Eigenvectors, Matrix Diago-
nalization, Norms and Inner products in Rn.
4. Course Summary: A lot of problems across engineering and management can be mod-
eled as optimization problems. Optimization problems come in various forms - Con-
tinuous and Discrete or Combinatorial Optimization being two of the most popular
ones. This course deals exclusively with Continuous optimization, and aims to intro-
duce both, theory and algorithms in Continuous optimization. The course will intro-
duce students to basic terminology in optimization, theory - conditions for existence
of points of optima(maxima/minima), and algorithms to solve optimization problems.
We will deal with both, linear and non-linear continuous optimization problems, fo-
cusing more on the latter.
5. Course Content:
(a) Basic terminology in Optimization: Local optima, Global Optima, Equivalence of
minimization and maximization, Convex sets and functions.
(b) Set Constrained/Unconstrained optimization theory: First and Second order Nec-
essary/Sufficient conditions for optima.
(c) Unconstrained optimization algorithms: Gradient Methods, Newton’s method,
Conjugate direction methods.
(d) Constrained non-linear optimization theory: Feasibility set and surface, Active
and inactive conditions, Lagrange multiplier, Karush-Kuhn-Tucker(KKT) condi-
tion.
(e) Special cases of Constrained Optimization: Linear programming, Basic feasible
solutions, Simplex method.
(f) Duality: Lagrangian duality, Geometric interpretation, Duality gap, Saddle point
interpretation.
(g) (If time permits) Constrained Optimization algorithms: Barrier & Penalty methods
6. References:
(a) An Introduction to Optimization, Chong and Zak, Fourth ed., Wiley.
(b) Linear and Nonlinear Programming, Luenberger and Ye, Fifth ed., Springer.
1

## Page 106

(c) Convex Optimization, Boyd and Vandenberghe, Cambridge University Press.
7. Course Outcomes: The student will be able to
(a) use the first and second order conditions to compute optima for small uncon-
strained optimization problems,
(b) implement line search, gradient descent, and Newton’s method for unconstrained
optimization,
(c) use the KKT conditions to derive conditions that an optima or a constrained non-
linear optimization problem has to satisfy,
(d) appreciate importance of optimization in Data Science applications.
8. Evaluation:
(a) Quizzes, Assignments/Lab exam - 30 −40%.
(b) In Sem 1 & 2 and/or Project - 30 −40%.
(c) End Sem - 30 −40%.
9. Program Outcomes:

## Page 107

MC312 – Modeling and Simulation
Course Title: Modelling and Simulation
Credit Structure(L-T-P-Cr): (3-0-2-4)
Course Code: MC312
Prerequisites (if any)/desired skill set: Introductory Calculus, Probability and Statistics, Linear
Algebra, and basic programming is required.
Level: Advanced Undergraduate

Course Placement: Modeling and Simulation is a core course offered to third year students of
B.Tech  Mathematics and Computing program.

Course format:  It is 3 hours’ lecture and 2 hours lab every week

Course content:  This course introduces students to the fundamentals of creating mathematical
models of physical systems and implementation on computers to analyze the system to gain insight
and make predictions. The different mathematical approaches to modeling covered in the course
can be characterized into differential and difference equation-based models, probability-based
models, including stochastic differential equations, cellular automata, event-based approaches, and
matrix-based models. The course is interdisciplinary and looks at systems from a modeling
perspective from physics, biology, social science, engineering, etc. Examples from different
disciplines follow each topic. The students are expected to test and create models of these systems
in the lab, gaining intuitive understanding and reporting on it following the complete modeling
approach. It may be the foundation for domain-based specialization electives such as Computational
Finance, Computational, and Systems Biology and Computational Physics, Computational Social
Science, etc.
Text Book:
Angela B. Shiflet & George W. Shiflet. Introduction to Computational Science: Modeling and
Simulation for the sciences, Princeton University Press, 2006.

Reference Book:
1) Sheldon M. Ross, Simulation, Academic Press, 2006.
2) Hiroki Sayama, Introduction to the Modeling and Analysis of Complex
Systems, Open SUNY Textbooks, Milne Library, 2015.
3) Taylor & Karlin, An Introduction to Stochastic Modeling, Academic Press,
1998.
4) S. Strogatz, Nonlinear Dynamic and Chaos, Oxford University Press,
2001.

## Page 108

Assessment method: Two in-semester examinations (40%), final examination (30%) and lab viva
(30%).

Course Outcomes:
After completing the course, the students would be able to create a relevant model for many
problems from science and engineering by extracting the necessary information regarding the
situation. They would also be able to define the different modeling terms by analyzing the system
or the available data. They would be able to implement the model on the computer and check for
its validity and the correctness of the assumptions present in the model. They will analyze the
outcomes (mainly through visualizations) and make predictions. They would also be able to
understand the limitations of their model and nuances in the computer modeling of systems.

  After succesfull completion of the course the student will have the ability to

 Apply the knowledge of mathematics and computer science to model complex problems in
science (P1,P2,P3,P12).
 Carry out model based investigations to derive insight into the problem at hand (P4).
 Communicate effectively through vivas, course presentation and report writing (P10)

## Page 110

MC313: Algebraic Structures
Course content
DA-IICT, B.Tech-MnC,
Objective:
A good way to study a system, natural or engineered, is to find its invariances because
these quantities define or label the system completely.
An invariant quantity is related
to a symmetry operation on the system.
Since the set of symmetry operations form a
Group, it becomes imperative for scientists and engineers to understand the basic ideas of
Group and its representations in vector spaces. This brings in the need to emphasize on
the structural aspects of algebra rather than only computational. The codes in Information
and Communication Technology are based on certain discrete groups and Fields. Hence the
objective of the course is to give a comprehensive understanding of these algebraic structures.
Emphasis is on the similarity of the various structures, i.e, homomorphism so that students
learn to map seemingly different problems using the underlying abstract algebraic structure.
Course Outline
1. Groups:
Basic Definitions, Subgroups, Cosets, Normal Subgroups, Lagrange’s Theorem, Iso-
morphism, Homomorphism, Quotient groups, Cayley’s Theorem.
2. Rings:
Definition, types of rings , zero divisors , integral domains, Fields, Characteristic of a
field.
3. The number system:
Examples of Rings and Fields in the number systems.
4. Polynomials:
Definition, The division Algorithm, Factorization.
5. Quotient Rings:
Homomorphisms, Ideals, Quotient rings, Quotient rings of Polynomial Rings, Factor-
ization and Ideals.
6. Field Extensions:
Simple Extensions, Degree of Extension, Splitting Fields, Finite Fields.
Books
1. Topics in Algebra
I.N. Herstein
2. Abstract Algebra, An Introduction
Thomas W. Hungerford
3. Algebra
Michael Artin
1

## Page 111

Grading
Two midsem test 20%+ 20% and an endsem 40%.
If possible Quiz will be conducted.
Quiz and attendance will comprise 20% of evaluation process.
Outcome:
Students learn the important algebraic structures of Groups and Fields which one encounters
in various areas of Science and Engineering. They learn homomorphism and the important
theorems of homomorphism which help in understanding and interpreting various aspects
of a given algebraic structure. Introduction to a variety of Rings and Fields help them to
generalize the structure of integers, real and complex numbers. Some of these fields like the
⟨Zn, ⊕⟩and ⟨Z∗
n, ⊗⟩are useful in coding and cryptography .

## Page 112

Principles of Economics (3-0-0-3)

Principles of Economics is a core course which includes elements of both Microeconomics and
Macroeconomics. It aims to develop an understanding of basic concepts of microeconomic
theory such as how a society allocates its scarce resources efficiently and also to develop an
in-depth understanding of how the macroeconomy works. The ways in which individuals make
their consumption decisions and firms decide on production are taken up when explaining
consumer preferences and the theory of the firm. Students would also get an introduction to
macroeconomic concepts such as national income accounting, unemployment and inflation.
Reference to monetary, fiscal and foreign exchange rate policies will help students to get a
greater understanding of the role of central banks and the government role in the economy.

Learning Objectives/Outcome:
Students will be able to reflect on their personal consumption decisions and understand better
how different market structures work particularly with respect to their implications for
consumer welfare. They should be able to interpret graphs and charts to explain consumer
choices, production decisions by firms and macroeconomic indicators like economic growth
and inflation. It is also expected that they would be able to appreciate global macroeconomic
developments and their influence on the domestic economy.

Text Book
1)    Principles of Microeconomics : Gregory Mankiw
(GM)
2)     Macroeconomics: Blanchard and Johnson (BJ)
Optional
Readings
1)    Intermediate Microeconomics : Hal Varian (HV)
2)    Macroeconomics: Dornbusch, Fischer, Startz
(DFS ),
3)    Macroeconomics: Gregory Mankiw (GM2).
4)     Principle of Economics: Samuelson (SAM)

Grading Policy:
The evaluation will consist of in-semester examination (30 per cent), end-semester examination
(40 marks) and quizzes (30 marks)

Course Objective: This course introduces design fundamentals necessary to implement VLSI

Semi & full custom designs: Placement & routing, Algorithms for Physical Design,
IO pads & ESD

 Analyze the operation and timing of CMOS circuits, including metastability, synchronization,
pipelining, and resource sharing in synchronous digital systems.
 Design and evaluate static and dynamic logic circuits such as CMOS, ratioed logic, domino
logic, and pass-transistor logic, including flip-flops and latches.
 Develop memory architectures with a focus on their layout, read/write mechanisms, and
integration.
 Apply algorithms for placement and routing in physical design.

## Page 120

Course Name: Technical Writing and Communication Skills
Course Code: PC 512
Instructor: Purbasha Das
 Autumn 2025
Course Outline
This course introduces first-year M. Tech and PhD students to the basics of technical writing
and communication skills that are essential in their professional life in the next 50-60 years.
The aim is to make the students understand the importance of effective communication for
professional success. A set of guidelines on several important aspects of technical writing as
well as presentations will be discussed in this course. Several non-technical and technical
writing exercises and slide-based presentations would be carried out for practical training of
the students. The students will be encouraged to improve their communication skills by
taking part in various group activities which will be organized throughout the semester.
Topics
A tentative list of topics is given below:
1. General guidelines of Technical Communication
2. Basic English grammar - selected topics
3. Developing Reading and Listening Skills
4. Writing and defending a Thesis
5. Publishing Papers in Journals
6. Writing and presenting a Paper in a Conference
7. Guidelines on reviewing and responding to reviews
8. Writing a Proposal
9. Writing a resume or CV and tips on good job interviews
10. Writing a Technical Report
11. Corporate Communications: Memos, Reports, etc.
12. Technical Presentation using slides
13. Poster Presentation techniques
14. Organizing references and developing a bibliography
15. Applying for a Patent
16. Ethics for Technical Communication
17. Using platform-independent public-domain software such as LibreOffice
Suite, Markdown, LATEX and Beamer.

Autumn 2025
PC613: Communication Skills
(Sem I, MSc IT)
This 4-credit course is designed to equip the MSc IT students with the essential skills for
effective communication and comprehension of ideas that will help them in their career as
IT professionals. The course will include sessions intended to improve the students’ writing
competence, ability to articulate ideas and think critically, presentation skills, and command
over English language. Exercises like group activities, writing tasks, class presentations, and
creative assignments will be combined with lectures on basic English grammar, reading skills,
and protocols of communication appropriate to a modern work culture.
Students are expected to dedicate time for completing take-home readings and writing
assignments. Apart from three hours of contact hours per week, the course will include two
hours of practical sessions per week dedicated to group activities and language skills.
Course Outcome:
At the end of the course, the students will be equipped with essential skills for writing and
speaking in English in everyday situations, and for communicating ideas as part of work, at
presentations and during interviews. During the course, the students will engage in
individual and group activities that will improve their writing and speaking abilities in
English, as well as their ability to comprehend and appreciate ideas and cultural artefacts
like literature, poetry and cinema.

Academic Yesr 2025-2026 (Autumn)
Instructor:
Dr. Supantha Pandit[supantha pandit@dau.ac.in]
Prerequisites:
Discrete Mathematics, C Programming
Slot/ Category:

Course Credits:
4 Credits (3-0-2)
Foundation for:
CS/IT stream of courses
Lectures:
TBD
TA Contact Info:
TBD
Course Description
This course aims to introduce the concept of data structures, their indispensability in
implementing algorithms, and how they aid in improving performance. Extensive cover-
age of the well-known and essential data structures such as arrays, linked lists, queues,
stacks, hash tables, binary search trees, AVL trees, red-black trees, 2-3-4 trees, heaps,
along with associated routines/algorithms such as dictionary operations, order statistics,
sorting will constitute the contents of the course.
Suggested Books
• Textbook:
– Data Structures and Algorithms by Aho, Hopcroft, and Ullman, Addison-
Wesley, 1999.
• Reference Books:
– Data Structures and Algorithms in C++/Java by Goodrich, Tamassia, and
Goldberg, Wiley, 2011
– Introduction to Algorithms by Cormen, Leiserson, Rivest, and Stein, PHI, 2010
Course Outcomes
After successful completion of the course, the student will have the ability to:
• Utilize appropriate simple data structures in problem-solving
• Understand data abstraction and order statistics
• Understand program efficiency through analysis of algorithms

Course Outline 
This course teaches use of Relational Database Management System to understand and 
solve a wide range of information storage and query processing problems in 
organizations ranging from large corporations to personal applications. The course 
combines the practical aspects of DBMS use with basic theory discussions about   
database design. As part of the lab assignments, students will learn to build and query 
the database using DBMS tool for the given problem domain. 
 
Text Books 
 
Silberschatz, Korth & Sudarshan, Database System Concepts, Seventh Edition, 2019, 
McGraw‐Hill 
 
Course Outcomes: 
The students will learn to store the data and process queries using RDBMS data model.  
Database will be designed and implemented using relational technology.

## Page 132

Title: Discrete Mathematics (core course)
Course code: SC612
Structure: 3-1-0-4
Course contents (tentative):
Mathematical logic & Proof techniques: Proposition logic, predicate logic and
quantifiers, logic operators, logic proposition Introduction to different standard proof
techniques
Basic discrete structures: Set operations, Functions, Relations and their properties,
Equivalence relations, Closure of relations, Partial orderings, Sequences and
summations, Matrices
Number Theory: Modular arithmetic, primes and representation of integers, linear
congruences
 Recurrence relations: Solving linear recurrence relations, Divide and conquer
algorithms and recurrence relations, its application to analysis of algorithms
Induction and recursion: Induction, strong induction, well-ordered property, recursion,
structural induction, and generalized induction;
Counting: Combinatorial Principles and Techniques, permutations and combinations,
binomial coefficients and identities, Pigeon hole principle, inclusion exclusion
principle
Discrete Probability: Basic probability theory, Bayes’ theorem, expected value and
variance
Graph Theory: Undirected graph, Representations, Handshaking lemma, Euler path
and circuit, Hamiltonian cycle, Complete graph, cycles, bipartite graph, planar
graphs, Euler formula, shortest path algorithm, Traveling salesman problem, trees.
properties of trees, spanning trees
Boolean Algebra: Boolean functions, logic gates, minimization expressions, K-maps

Course Outcomes:
• Logic Proofs: Develop the ability to construct and analyze formal logical
proofs using propositional and predicate logic.
• Basics of Counting: Master fundamental counting principles including
permutations, combinations, and the principle of inclusion-exclusion.
• Mathematical Induction: Understand and apply mathematical induction to
prove statements about integers and sequences.
• Recursive Algorithms: Design and analyze recursive algorithms for solving
problems such as sorting, searching, and graph traversal.
• Boolean Algebra: Explore Boolean functions, truth tables, Boolean
expressions, and their applications in digital circuits and computer science.

## Page 133

• Discrete Probability: Gain proficiency in applying probability theory to discrete
scenarios, including basic probability distributions and expected values.

These outcomes encompass essential topics covered in a typical discrete
mathematics course, providing students with foundational knowledge and problem-
solving skills crucial for further studies in mathematics, computer science, and related
fields.
Text book:
• Discrete Mathematics and its Applications - Kenneth.H. Rosen
• Elements of Discrete Mathematics C. L. Liu Publisher: Tata McGrawHill

Grading policy (tentative): The grade earned by a student will depend on (1) the
total marks earned by him in all the exams conducted over the entire semester, and
(2) active participation in the class discussions and labs. The weightage of each part
is as follows:
Insem 1: 20%
Insem 2: 20%
Endsem: 40%
Tutorials/Lecture Participation/ Assignments: 20 %

IT603 - Introduction to programming
Course Placement: Autumn 2025 – Core MSc IT
Course Format: It is 3 lectures and one lab every week
Prerequisites (if any): None
Credit Structure (L-T-P-Cr): 3-0-2-4
Instructor’s Name with email: Ankush Chander (ankush_chander@dau.ac.in)
Course Objective: Course is designed as a first timer introduction to programming. It assumes no prior exposure to
programming. Course aims at imparting students solid understanding of not only programming language features but
also fundamentals knowledge that" transferable to other languages also.   
Course Content:
Introduction to Linux environment - Computer system, Shell , Command line Interface,  Standard I/O, System Path
Introduction to Programming – Introduction to Modern C++, concept of program flow, why modern C++ without OOP is
C, Hello world, Program structure,  Compilation process
Data types and Concept of Variables – Data types, Variable, Static variables, global variables
Program Control – Logical operators, bitwise operators, functions, Conditional constructs
Array, Pointers, Structures – Arrays, pointers, array of characters, multi-dimensional arrays, pointers to functions,
structures and classes
Dynamic Memory Allocation – creating variables on the free-store, memory management
Modern C++: Templates – Generic programming, Template structure and definition
Modern C++: STL – std::string, STL algorithms
Suggested Textbook/references:
Assessment Method:
Grading Policy: Relative grading with the following slabs:
Course outcome:
After taking this course, students will be able to write portable, efficient application programs in C++. They will also be
able to navigate Linux environments fluently and take up new programming languages with relative ease.
1. Think C++ by Allen B Downey
2. the C++ Programming Language by Bjourne Stroustrup
3. UNIX : Concepts and Applications, Sumitabha Das
4. Computer Systems: A Programmer's Perspective, Randal E. Bryant and David R. O'Hallaron

In-Sem Exam: 30
End Sem Exam: 40

## Page 137

Statistical methods –Data Science

Course Placement: It’s a core course for students in Masters in Data Science Programme.
Course format: Three 50 minutes’ lectures every week.
Lab: 2 hours every week

Course Content:
Data summarization: Data types, Data visualization and summarization with the help of tables and
descriptive measures.
Probability: Statistical interpretation of probability, Bayes’ theorem and its applications.
Standard probability models: Binomial, Poisson, Uniform, Exponential and Normal, Data generation from
different models using Monte Carlo method.
Sampling: Concepts of population and sample, Sampling distributions of sample mean and sample
proportion, Central limit theorems, Illustration of sampling distribution using Monte Carlo simulation.
statistical inference
Statistical estimation: Point and interval estimation, Methods of estimation (method of moments &
maximum likelihood method)
Statistical testing of hypotheses: Null and alternative hypotheses, Significance testing using p-value,
Standard parametric and non-parametric tests
Regression Analysis: Linear and Logistic regression
Time Series Analysis: Components of time series, Determination of Trend and Seasonal components
Special topics:
Resampling technique: Bootstrap
For all topics course notes will be provided by the instructor. In addition, the following books may be
used as ready references.
References:
1. Statistics for Business & Economics – Anderson et al., Cengage Learning (2018)
2. Introduction to Data Science -- Rafael A. Irizarry (CRC Press but freely available in
https://leanpub.com/datasciencebook)
3. An introduction to probability theory and its application (Volume -1) —William Feller (Wiley
series in probability and statistics)
4. Statistical Inference – George Casella and Roger L. Berger (Cengage Learning India)
5. An introduction to the Bootstrap—Bradley Efron and Robert J. Tibshirani (Chapman Hall/CRC
Press)

## Page 138

6. Introductory Statistics with R – Peter Daalgard, Springer (2008)

Assessment method: Quizzes, project presentation, and an end-term examination
Course Outcomes: After completion of this course, students will
●
Learn the fundamental concepts and the basic tools of Data Analytics
●
Learn the implementation of the analytics tools using R
●
Acquire the skill to analyze data in a systematic and logical manner to answer important
research questions arising in different disciplines, especially in the field of agriculture
●
Learn how to attach a measure of uncertainty to the inference drawn from data
●
Get a feel for data driven decision making

DS603 – Data Structures and Algorithms in Python

Course Placement: Data Structures and Algorithms is a core course for first-year students of the
MSc Data Science program.

Course format: It is 3 hours of lecture and 2 hours of lab practical every week.

Prerequisite: None expected
Course Content: Data Structures and Algorithms in Python introduces students to the
fundamental concepts and techniques essential for efficient problem-solving in computer
science. Through lectures, coding labs, and problem-solving sessions, students will develop a
solid foundation in data structures and algorithms, preparing them for more advanced
coursework and real-world applications in data science.
Text Books:
 Data Structures and Algorithms in Python by Michael Goodrich, Roberto Tamassia, Michael
Goldwasser, Wiley Publication
 Python crash course: a hands-on project based introduction to programming, 2nd Edition, by
Eric Matthews, San Francisco No Starch Press 2019.
 Learn Python the hard way: a very simple introduction to the terrifyingly beautiful world of
computers and code, 3rd Edition, by Zed Shaw, Upper Saddle River: Addison-Wesley, 2014
Other Resources:
 www.python.org

Assessment method:
•
Pop-up Quizzes: 10%
•
Lab Assignments: 15%
•
Project: 20%
•
First In-Semester Exam: 10%
•
Second In-Semester Exam: 20%
•
End-Semester Exam: 25%

Course Outcomes:
After successful completion of the course,
•
Students will learn the Python programming language and tools such as Jupyter
Notebook through hands-on exercises.
•
Students will be able to solve logical problems by implementing the solutions using the
Python programming language.
•
Students will be able to store and retrieve data using Python data structures such as lists
and dictionaries.
•
Students will be able to implement different general data structures, such as linked lists,
stacks, queues, and trees and implement CRUD operations on these data structures
•
Students will implement different searching and sorting algorithms and perform the time
and space complexity analysis.

DS604, Introduction to Data Management (Autumn’2025)
Course Placement: M.Sc. (Data Science), Core, Semester–I
Course format: 3 Lecture hours and 2 hours Lab
Course Instructor: PM Jat, 2203, Faculty Block 2
Course Content:
Introduction to Data Management is designed to provide students with a foundational
understanding databases that spans to
(1) Data, databases, data models, data representations and operations on them. Roles
of metadata, and database management systems
(2) Relational Model as database implementation model, its concepts, key constructs,
and manipulations
(3) Manipulation Operations through Relational Algebra visa-a-vis SQL
(4) ER Modeling: Common artifacts of ER Modeling, and their uses in depicting a
database design in an implementation independent manner.
(5) Formal approaches for Designing and evaluation of relational databases
(6) Techniques to created database server hosted programs that run in the close
proximity of the data providing efficient execution and provide highest level of
shareability of the functionality
(7) Technique for efficiently storing the data that provide efficient access for
answering the queries
(8) Concepts of Query Optimization and Execution for relational systems.
(9) Concerns and Techniques to address those concerns while concurrent Transaction
Processing and Technics that are used in dealing with concurrent
Labs and Assignments:
During Lab hours, students practice concepts learned in theory and would be asked to
design and implement a database system for a mid-size real-life scenario.
Text Books:
1. Fundamentals of Database Systems, Ramez Elmasri and Shamkant B. Navathe, Pearson Education
2. Database System Concepts by Avi Silberschatz, Henry F. Korth, S. Sudarshan, TMH
Evaluation plan:
1. Two Mid Semester Exams: 30%
2. Project: 20%
3. Lab Evaluation: 20%
4. End Semester evaluation: 30%
Course Outcomes:
The database is one of the core competency areas for data science students. Through this course,
students develop the ability to

Design and implement Database systems.

## Page 143


Write and solve complex queries systematically

Write programs that access databases for further processing

Course Description:
This course provides a comprehensive introduction to the fundamentals of machine learning, which
are essential for first-year M.Sc. Data Science students. The curriculum covers classical machine
learning techniques, including supervised and unsupervised learning, decision trees, support vector
machines, clustering, and outlier analysis. Students will learn the foundational concepts of learning
frameworks, model evaluation, and validation. The course emphasizes practical applications and
hands-on experience through projects and lab sessions, preparing students to understand and
implement basic machine learning algorithms and methodologies.

Suggested Books:
●​
Data Mining and Machine Learning: Fundamental Concepts and Algorithms by Zaki and
Meira, 2nd Edition, Cambridge University Press [2020].
●​
Machine Learning with Python Cookbook by Kyle Gallatin and Chris Albon, 2nd Edition.
●​
Understanding Machine Learning: From Theory to Algorithms by Shwartz and David [2022]
Course Outcomes:
After completing the course, the student should be able to -
●​
Understand the foundations of Machine Learning (ML).
●​
Develop skills in using recent ML techniques to solve practical problems.
●​
Gain experience in doing an independent study and research
●​
Apply all of the above in Python (using Python ML Stack: Scikit-learn, Numpy, Pandas)

## Page 148

DS605: Fundamentals of Machine Learning
Academic Year 2025-26 (Autumn)

Guidelines for Lab Assignments, Development Challenges, and Course Project
Lab Assignments
●​
Each student must select a domain of personal interest and collect a large dataset related to it.
●​
Additional data scraping is required to enrich the dataset for use in building models during the
semester.
●​
By the second week, students must register their domain of interest and team members.
○​
Note: No two team members can select the same domain.
○​
Students from different teams may work on similar domains, but no two students may use the
same dataset.
●​
Each lab session will be structured as follows:
○​
The first hour will include a concept explanation and demo by the TAs.
○​
Assignments will be released after the demo.
○​
Students will have 2 hours to complete and submit their work (Colab/Jupyter notebooks) on
Google Classroom.
●​
Submissions must be made from within the M.Sc. Data Science lab only. Remote submissions are not
permitted.
●​
Late submissions will be penalized:
○​
Submissions beyond 6 hours of assignment release will receive no credit.
●​
A total of six lab assignments will be conducted during the course.

Development Challenges and Course Project
●​
Students will form teams of four members. Each team will:
○​
Participate in five timed ML Development Challenges (each lasting 100 minutes).
○​
Complete an End-to-End Course Project based on one of the domains previously worked on
individually: define their project problem, gather relevant data, and develop a solution.
●​
Team evaluation will account for 30% of the total course marks. Choose team members carefully.
●​
Teams will remain fixed for the entire semester; no changes will be allowed after registration.
○​
With 65 students, there will be 16 teams (one team may have five members).
○​
Team registration will open in Week 2 and remain open for three days. Registration will be via
a Google Form.
●​
During the challenges, relative grading will be used based on the highest-performing team.

Page No 5

## Page 149

DS605: Fundamentals of Machine Learning
Academic Year 2025-26 (Autumn)

Course Project Details
●​
Each team will choose one thematic domain, such as:
○​
Finance, E-commerce, Healthcare, Pharmaceuticals, Sports, Entertainment,
Renewable Energy, Oil & Gas, Automobile, Agriculture, FMCG, Security, Social Media,
Supply Chain, or any other relevant and engaging domain.
●​
Each group must:
○​
Define a unique problem within the selected domain.
○​
Use reliable and preferably public data sources.
○​
Ensure no other group is working on the same dataset within the same domain.
○​
Enrich the dataset, if possible, through additional data collection.
○​
Every team member must be able to explain all aspects of their course project
(concepts, code, methodology, etc.).

Submission and Evaluation
●​
One group member must submit the final Course Project on Google Classroom or GitHub
Classroom.
●​
Evaluation will be conducted through a Chalk Talk, which includes:
○​
Problem definition
○​
Code review
○​
Dataset scale and quality
○​
Tools and technologies used
○​
Methodology
○​
Novelty and originality of the approach
○​
All group members should be ready to answer any questions related to the project.
●​
Late project submissions (within 24 hours of the deadline) will incur a 20% penalty.

Plagiarism Policy
Plagiarism will result in zero marks for Lab Assignments, Challenges, and the Course Project.
Plagiarism includes:
●​
Copying code from any external source.
●​
Submitting work not written personally by the student.
●​
Any suspected violation will be strictly penalized.

## Page 150

IT643 - Software Design and Testing
Course Placement: Autumn 2025 – Core MSc IT
Course Format: It is 3 lectures and one lab every week
Prerequisites (if any): Awareness of Object oriented programming
Credit Structure (L-T-P-Cr): 3-0-2-4
Instructor’s Name with email: Ankush Chander (ankush_chander@dau.ac.in)
Course Objective: 
Course aims to impart students know-how of creating modular, flexible, and reusable software, by applying object-
oriented design principles and guidelines. And, you will be able to communicate these designs in a visual notation
known as Unified Modelling Language (UML).
Course Content:
- Evolution of programming- Programming Paradigms
- Software design vs Software Architecture
- Role of design in software process
- Object Oriented principles: Classes (Data + behaviour), Categories of objects, Encapsulation, Abstraction,
Generalization, Decomposition
- Modelling using CRC(Class Responsibilities Collaborators) cards and UML diagrams
- Design principles - Coupling and Cohesion, Separation of Concerns, Information Hiding, Conceptual Integrity
- Software Testing: Basic concepts and preliminaries,
- Design and Code Inspections to Reduce Errors in Program Development,
- Functional Testing (Black Box, White Box, Mutation Regression)
- Debugging,
- Verification of Software Systems, Testing of Web GUI and Mobile Apps
- Non-functional Testing.
Suggested Textbook/references:
1. Head First Object-Oriented Analysis and Design
2. Software Design and Architecture Specialization
Assessment Method:
- Lab +Group Project assignments: 50% (group size will be a maximum of 4 students)
- Mid-Sem Exam: 25%
- End Sem Exam: 25%
Grading Policy: Relative grading with the following slabs:
- >90 = AA
- 80-90 = AB
- 65-79 = BB
- 50-64 = BC
- 40-50 = CC
- <39 = CD
Course outcome:
Upon completion, students will be equipped with the advanced principles, modern techniques, and practical skills
required to architect software that is inherently testable, maintainable, reflecting the core tenets of creating software that
is "safe from bugs, easy to understand, and ready for change".

## Page 151

Web Services & Service-Oriented Architecture (IT644)
Course placement: Web Services & Service-oriented Architecture (WSSOA) is a core course for the
MScIT program in Semester-3.
Course format: 3 hours’ lecture and 2-hour laboratory every week.
Course content:
The WSSOA course provides an in-depth exploration of modern distributed computing paradigms,
focusing on the design, development, and deployment of interoperable services across heterogeneous
platforms. The course covers foundational concepts such as XML, JSON, and RESTful principles, along
with advanced topics including SOAP-based services, WSDL, UDDI, and security standards like WS-
Security  and  OAuth.  Students  will  gain  hands-on  experience  with  API  design,  micro-services
architectures, cloud-based service deployment, and integration techniques, while critically examining
scalability, reliability, and performance considerations in enterprise environments. Through case
studies and lab work, learners will bridge theoretical understanding with practical implementation,
preparing them for research or industry roles in building robust, scalable, and secure service-oriented
solutions.
Books and Literature:
• Service-Oriented Architecture: Concepts, Technology, and Design by Thomas E.
• SOA Principles of Service Design by T Erl.
• Service Design Patterns: Fundamental Design Solutions for SOAP/WSDL and RESTful Web Services
by Robert Daigneau
• Patterns of Enterprise Application Architecture by Martin Fowler
• Enterprise Integration Patterns: Designing, Building, and Deploying Messaging Solutions by Gregor
Hohpe & Bobby Woolf
• Building Microservices by Sam Newman
Assessment Method: 6-8 lab assignments, a group project, class tests, and end-semester examination.
Course Outcomes:
By the end of the Web Services and Service-Oriented Architecture course, postgraduate students will
be  able  to  design,  implement,  and  deploy  interoperable,  scalable,  and  secure  service-based
applications  that  integrate  seamlessly  across  heterogeneous  systems.  They  will  demonstrate
proficiency in creating both RESTful and SOAP-based services, applying industry standards such as
WSDL, UDDI, XML, JSON, WS-Security, and OAuth, and utilizing micro-services and cloud platforms for
distributed deployments. Students will be capable of critically evaluating service design patterns,
ensuring reliability, scalability, and maintainability in enterprise environments. Furthermore, they will
develop the analytical and practical skills to architect service-oriented solutions that address real-
world  business  challenges,  bridging  the  gap  between  theoretical  concepts  and  professional
implementation.
After successful completion of the course the student will have the ability to,

## Page 152

●Understand, analyze, plan, manage, and implement heterogeneous software systems (P1,
P2, P12).
●Apply software design principles for cloud implementation / handling software projects
using various tools and techniques (P3, P5, P11).
●Plan, monitor, track, and review software projects in a large group setup (P9, P11).
●Create software architecture and related documents, detailed project plans, schedules,
along with necessary software implementation strategy. (P10, P11)

Introduction & Foundations
• Course overview, objectives, and industry relevance of Web Services & SOA
• Distributed systems and the evolution towards SOA
• Service-orientation principles — abstraction, contract, reusability, autonomy
• Overview of Web service types — REST vs SOAP
• XML and JSON as data exchange formats
Core Web Service Technologies
• SOAP fundamentals — message structure, processing model
• WSDL — service description and structure
• UDDI — service registry and discovery concepts
• REST fundamentals — resources, URIs, statelessness, CRUD mapping
• HTTP methods, status codes, and content negotiation in REST
• Comparative study: SOAP vs REST — use cases and trade-offs
SOA Architecture & Patterns
• SOA reference architecture and components
• Enterprise Service Bus (ESB) — concepts and role in integration
• Service design principles and granularity decisions
• Service composition and orchestration (BPEL, BPMN)
• SOA design patterns — inventory, composition, messaging patterns

## Page 153

• Integration patterns — request/response, publish/subscribe, aggregator, mediator
• Case studies of SOA implementations in enterprise systems
Security, QoS & Advanced Topics
• Security challenges in web services
• WS-Security — SOAP security extensions
• OAuth 2.0, JWT, and API key management for REST services
• Reliability and transactional integrity in services (WS-ReliableMessaging, XA)
• Performance optimization techniques for web services
• Cloud-based SOA — deploying services to AWS/Azure/GCP
Microservices & Contemporary Trends
• Microservices architecture vs SOA — similarities, differences, convergence
• Service discovery, load balancing, and API gateways (Netflix OSS, Kong, NGINX)
• Containerization for service deployment — Docker fundamentals
• Orchestration of services using Kubernetes

Course name and code: Web and Mobile Development (IT645)
Course placement: core course for M.Sc.(IT) Sem III

Course objective: The objective of this course is to provide an overview of the building blocks of a
functional webpage and to understand the frontend and backend technologies that power the modern
websites and mobile applications. It will help learn skills to develop dynamic webpages and integrate APIs
through Javascript-enabled frontend framework, ReactJS and backend of NodeJS. It aims at teaching a
hybrid mobile application development technology, React Native for iOS as well as Android operating
systems.

comprising the MERN stack and React Native. Detailed course content is listed below.

## Page 157

DS-805 (Machine Learning System Engineering)
Credit Structure (L-T-P-Cr): 3 0 2 4
Content:
 Unit 1 – ML Systems, Collaboration & Data Acquisition
●​ Overview of ML System Lifecycle and MLOps in production systems​

●​ Object-oriented Python for ML applications​

●​ Git/GitHub workflows: branching, merging, pull requests, code reviews​

●​ Web scraping & automation (Beautiful Soup, Scrapy)​

●​ Ethical and legal considerations in data acquisition​

Unit 2 – Containerization & Kubernetes for ML
●​ Docker for reproducible ML environments​

●​ Building, managing, and optimizing Docker images​

●​ Kubernetes ecosystem: pods, deployments, services, scaling​

●​ Deploying and managing ML workloads on Kubernetes clusters​

Unit 3 – Data Pipelines, APIs & Cloud Deployment
●​ Building and orchestrating data pipelines (Apache Airflow/Luigi)​

●​ Scheduling and monitoring ETL workflows​

●​ RESTful API development (Django/FastAPI) with MVC, ORM, and security​

●​ Cloud platforms for ML (AWS SageMaker, Azure ML, GCP AI Platform)​

●​ Kubernetes on cloud for scalable ML services​

●​ CI/CD for ML, testing, monitoring, logging, A/B testing, cost optimization​

## Page 158

Unit 4 – Security, Best Practices & Industry Integration
●​ Security & compliance in MLOps (GDPR, ISO)​

●​ Case studies from e-commerce, finance, healthcare​

●​ Lab: End-to-end corporate-style ML project from data acquisition to Kubernetes
deployment​

●​ Capstone mini-project with CI/CD integration​

●​ Industry mentor guest lecture​

●​ Certification readiness (AWS Certified ML Specialty or equivalent)

Suggested Text/s:

1.​ Designing Machine Learning Systems – Chip Huyen​

●​ Why: This is one of the most practical books on building and deploying ML systems
at scale. Covers ML lifecycle, MLOps, data pipelines, experimentation, deployment,
and monitoring in industry settings.​

●​ Industry Relevance: Written from a product and engineering perspective, with case
studies from leading tech companies.​

2.​ Kubernetes for Machine Learning – Emmanuel Raj​

●​ Why: Hands-on approach to running, scaling, and managing ML workloads with
Kubernetes. Includes examples with Docker, Kubeflow, and cloud platforms.​

●​ Industry Relevance: Perfect for bridging the gap between data science and
production infrastructure.

Course structure:
1.​ 30% mid semester exam,
2.​ 30% viva(alternate Wednesday, starting from 20th),
3.​ 40% assignments/project

Course Details:

This course offers a comprehensive introduction to the fundamental principles of statistical
analysis and data analytics, carefully designed for direct application within agricultural sciences.
Students will develop skills in the systematic collection, management, analysis, and interpretation
of various types of agricultural data, utilizing modern statistical software. The curriculum covers
key topics such as descriptive statistics, probability distributions, statistical inference,
experimental design principles, regression analysis and time series analysis. Emphasis will
be placed on integrating real-world agricultural case studies and practical exercises throughout
the course. The main goal is to enable students to shift from traditional, often guesswork-based
farming methods toward evidence-based, data-driven, and predictive agriculture. Students are
expected to be familiar with the Python programing language basics for agricultural data analysis.

Textbooks:

1. Statistics for Business & Economics - Anderson et al., Cengage Learning (2018)
2. Think Stats - Allen B Downey (2011)
3. Statistics Using Python - Oswald Campesato  (2023)
4. A Text Book of Agricultural Statistics - R. Rangaswamy (2022)

Course Outcomes: After successful completion of this course, students will be able to

 Collect, organize, and summarize agricultural data effectively.
 Apply descriptive and inferential statistical methods to various agricultural
datasets.
 Design and analyze agricultural experiments using appropriate statistical
techniques.
 Utilize Python for data analysis and visualization.
 Acquire the skill to analyze data in a systematic and logical manner to answer
important research questions arising in the field of agriculture
 Interpret statistical results to inform data-driven decisions in agriculture.
 Communicate statistical findings clearly and effectively
 Recognize the ethical considerations and limitations of statistical analysis in agri-
analytics.

Assessment method: Quizzes/Assignments, project presentation, Lab work and semester
examinations.

Grading Policy:
30% Quizzes/Assignments
30% Project/Tests/Viva
40% Final exam
This grading policy is subject to change and the final grading policy will be updated once
the class starts.

## Page 160

Tentative Lecture Schedule

Sl.No.Description
No. of
Lectures
1.
Introduction to Data and Statistics in Agriculture
5
Basics of Statistics: Population vs. Sample, Parameters vs. Statistics, Data types, Types of agricultural data:
field data, remote sensing data, weather data, market data, soil data, livestock data.
Data Collection and Organization: Sources of agricultural data (sensor networks, drones, satellite imagery,
field surveys, government databases).
Data Visualization and Exploratory Data Analysis: Data visualization and summarization with the help of
tables and the important descriptive measures.

2.
Probability Theory and Distributions
5
Concepts of probability (both objective and subjective). Statistical interpretation of probability, Bayes’
theorem and its applications in solving real-life decision problems, Standard probability distributions:
Standard probability models (Binomial, Poisson, Uniform, Exponential, Gaussian), Probability distributions
of transforms of random variables, Data generation from probability distributions using Monte Carlo
method.

3.
Sampling Techniques & Distributions
7
Basic sampling concepts (population, sample, sampling frame, sampling unit) and probability sampling
methods (simple random sampling, stratified sampling, systematic sampling, cluster sampling). Concept of
sampling distribution. Central Limit Theorem and its importance. Sampling distribution of the mean.
Illustration of sampling distribution using Monte Carlo simulation

4.
Statistical inference
10
Statistical estimation: Point and interval estimation, Methods of estimation (method of moments &
maximum likelihood method)
Hypothesis testing: Null and alternative hypotheses, Significance testing using p-value, parametric and
non-parametric tests
Analysis of Variance:  One-Way ANOVA: Comparing means of three or more independent groups (e.g.,
different crop varieties).
Two-Way ANOVA: Examining the effect of two independent categorical variables and their interaction (e.g.,
fertilizer type and irrigation level on yield).
Post-hoc tests (e.g., Tukey HSD) for multiple comparisons.

5.
Correlation & Regression Analysis:
6

Concept of correlation: Strength and direction of relationship, Pearson's correlation coefficient (r),
Spearman's rank correlation (for non-parametric data), Scatter plots for visualizing correlation.
Introduction to regression: Predicting one variable from another, simple and multiple linear regression,
Least Squares method for estimating regression coefficients, Coefficient of determination, Polynomial
regression, Logistic regression

6.
Time Series Analysis
5

Components of time series data (trend, seasonality, cycle, irregularity), Basic forecasting methods (e.g.,
moving averages), Applications in agricultural market prediction, weather pattern analysis.

7.
Design of Experiments:
4

Principles of Experimental Design: Randomization, Replication, and Local Control, Importance of good
experimental design in agricultural research.
Common Experimental Designs in Agriculture:
Completely Randomized Design (CRD), Randomized Block Design (RBD), Latin Square Design (LSD),
Factorial Experiments, Split-plot and Strip-plot designs, Analysis of data from these designs using ANOVA.

8.
Bootstrap estimation of Bias and Standard Error (if time permits)

Course Description
This course provides foundational mathematical concepts required for M.Sc. Agriculture
Analytics students who have studied mathematics up to secondary (10th) level. The
emphasis is on geometric intuition, concrete examples from agriculture and simple com-
putational skills to prepare students for further coursework in analytics and statistics.
Course Objectives
1. Reinforce essential mathematical ideas required for quantitative courses in analytics.
2. Provide geometric and intuitive understanding of vectors, functions, calculus and
probability.
3. Equip students with basic skills to interpret and manipulate simple mathematical
models relevant to agricultural data.

## Page 175

1
Course PC 748

INTRODUCTION TO DESIGN HISTORY

Instructor - Prof. Madhumita Mazumdar

COURSE INSTRUCTOR-   Prof. Madhumita Mazumdar

Course Objective :  This course will introduce students to the emergent field of
Design History or Design Studies as a part of a broader attempt to instill in
students a critical engagement with their discipline.  It will map the evolution of
interest in ‘historical’ questions related to Design both as practice and as
discipline. But before that we will try to understand the many meanings of
Design in use and ask which one of these helps us understand the true nature of
our practice.

Course Structure:

The course will introduce students to the range of approaches to the study of the
History of Design- particularly conventional, narrative approaches to Design
History and conceptual and critical approaches to the same.

Students would be able to make the distinction between practice and discipline
even if both are interlinked in order to understand how historians have
approached the subject of their study. The module will  also include an important
connecting thread in the History of Design- that of the larger economic, social,
political and cultural contexts within which Design took shape both as practice

## Page 176

2
and as pedagogy. So there will be two entangled strands of historical inquiry we
will follow- one following the development of Design and the other looking at the
global and national contexts that shaped it.

These global contexts are indicated by such historical periodizations such as the
Industrial Revolution, the first Great War and the  rise of Communism in Russia,
the inter-war years,  the rise of Fascism the Cold War era, the era of Mass
Consumption, the Counter-Culture and the contemporary era of Globalization.

The final segment of the course will turn to India and look at colonial context in
which India found itself during the period of the Industrial Revolution. We
will see how the first consciousness of an Indian-designed and made products
was emerging in the context of the Swadeshi movement in 1905-1911, the
impact of the emergence of Gandhi in sensitizing us to the power of Design in
nationalist politics and finally we will look into the ways in which both modern
western Design practice and pedagogy as well as our own craft traditions and
material culture served to shape the context within which India’s first modern
Design school was established in 1961.

Course Outcome : Toward the end of the course, we will try to understand how
Design history helps us engage with questions of form, function and aesthetics in
completely different ways and generate new ways of looking at the products and
services they consume as part of our everyday lives. They will seek to
understand how does Design History prompt us to question the larger
implications of what we buy or use in terms of the impacts these have on human
health and wellbeing, on the built environment and the planet at large.

There will be continuous evaluation of students through class participation,
individual presentations and a final research paper, seminar or in-class
examination.

Selected Readings from:

3

Nikolaus Pevsner, Pioneers of Modern Design (1936; revised ed. 1960)
John Heskett, Industrial Design (1980) and Design: A Very Short Introduction
(2002)
Jonathan Woodham, Twentieth Century Design (1997)
Victor Margolin, World History of Design (2015, 2 vols.)
H.K. Kumar Vyas : Alternative Histories of Design (essays)
S. Balaram : Thinking Design

COURSE : APPROACHES TO CULTURE AND COMMUNICATION
COURSE INTRUCTOR : VISHVAJIT PANDYA
PC 721

Honour Code
Academic dishonesty cheating, plagiarism or any kind of deceit- will not be tolerated,
and will result in a zero for the assignment. If you have any questions about what
constitutes plagiarism, please ask. All ideas and words that you did not generate
yourself must be cited in your papers. I do not anticipate this scenario for the work
required in this class, but in extreme cases I reserve the right to assign a failing grade
for the course or a specific assignment.

LEARN TO TAKE LECTURE NOTES AND KEEP A SMALL NOTE BOOK TO
WRITE IDEAS AND WORDS THAT YOU DO NOT UNDERSAND AND LOOK
UP THE MEANING OR ASK IN CLASS. BUT MAKE A NOTE WHERE THE
QUESTION ORIGINATED FROM.

COURSE OBJECTIVES

Communication between any form of transmitter and recipient is not just a
matter of ‘hardware’ and ‘software’. Communication, what it achieves, how it is
interpreted, what is understood, conveyed and represented involves social ideology,
cultural techniques and whole society’s historical context. After all communication
does not take place in vacuum! By the 18th century, mainly in western world,
development of print culture lead to creation and evolution of a modern ‘public culture’.
This included process of identifying, weighing, and discussing the issues of the day in
ways hardly possible before the print revolution.

This multidisciplinary approach seminar course students could explore the
inter relationship of orality, writing. Printing, the visual image, theatre and music, by
encouraging students to research and discuss the interaction between various modalities

## Page 179

2

of communication within Indian society and history. We intend to examine how culture
communicates and communication creates culture with particular intend to consider
relationship between image and sound.

This course based on a seminar format. It is aimed to develop conceptual
schemes and patterns by which historically (changing times-including future and its
base in past) ideas objects and actions have been developed and or evolved to
communicate within Indian culture and its contextual positioning with other cultures as
broad as colonialism and globalization.  This locating in understanding may include
broadly language socialization, literacy, music and the visual arts, the power of
language, miscommunication, and universal and culture-specific properties of human
communication as embodied in ritual performances, organized spectacles and
structured events in public spaces.
Utilizing the ideas from Anthropology, Sociology, Semiotics History and
Cultural Studies the course first will provide some understanding of basic
conceptual and analytical foundations. So actual context and its depiction
(ethnographic case) will be presented to you along with analytical frameworks that
can be derived from it (theoretical)
The course expects you to be open minded, critical and think about society and culture
beyond India. You are expected to acquire basic sensibility to various assumptions
about how and why societies communicate. The readings for each section will be dealt
in the class as discussion sessions with student participation and presentation. Do not
expect that lecture would solely be based on the readings. Readings are just a base to
back up the ideas presented by the faculty.

COURSE CONTENT AND THEMES

## Page 180

3

With globalization, the Internet, and the general shrinking of time and space, cross-
cultural interaction has become a necessity in people’s everyday lives. This course is
designed to interrogate different aspects of cross-cultural communication and cultural
differences: family life, social relationships, the workplace, government, education,
gender, romance, and religion. Throughout exploring these topics, we will strive to
engage in personal self-reflection, hands-on experience, and to understand the
connections to larger social structures.
Specifically, the goals of this course are: 1) to describe, learn, and see
common and important patterned social and cultural differences, 2) to provide a
space for students to reflect on their own personal experience with cultural
difference, and 3) to encourage students to engage with different cultures in a
hands-on way and to meaningfully experience cross-cultural communication.

EVALUATION : CONTINUOUS EVALUATION AND RESEARCH PAPER
● You are expected to attend the lectures and participate in all the discussions
and undertake the assignments based on readings, above and beyond the
provided references. Your active and informed participation in the class has
impact on your grade. Individuals must develop the capacity to think critically,
support their point of view by well-informed arguments and creative research
and observations from real contexts.
● Sequence of lectures will cover some essential ideas that the course intends to
impart to you.
● The lectures would then be followed by discussion around readings that
would help you to understand the basic ideas in relation to concrete specific
cultural case.
● .
● The ‘research project’ has to be turned in by individual student as a
researched paper (double spaced typed of 10 to 12 pages, 12pt.font, normal
margins) focusing on a theme provided by the faculty. You are expected to
consult the curse coordinators to develop your research topic. This project has
to be presented by individual in the classroom as a presentation.

## Page 181

4

● Your final project and presentation would be worth 55 % and remaining
45% of your grade will be accounted by your class participation,
assignments and presentations as well as pop quiz.

## Page 185

Course Abstract: PC 722 FOD 1 for MDes CD

1

COURSE FILE

PC722: Fundamentals of Design - I (Drawing, Observation & Composition)

Credit Structure: 3-0-4-5
Program: MDes (Communication Design)
Year: 1
Semester: 1
Duration: 18 Days (Full Day – Lecture + Studio)
Timing: 9 AM – 6 PM

Course Instructor: Anirban Dutta Gupta + Visiting Faculty

Couse Placement: Core course for MDes (CD) Semester 1. This course is composed of two
parts – part A: Drawing and Observation and Part B: Composition. Part A and Part B is taken
consecutively. This is a foundational course in design.

Course Format: Module based. The course is taught as a combination of Lectures and Studio
sessions. Lectures introduce concepts, which are then analysed and explored via studio
sessions and assignments. Lecture and studio is integrated and in certain cases can overlap.
Teaching is through hand-on learning and through learning-by-doing and via real-time
demonstrations and guidance by faculty members.

Course Objective and Content:
Design is an interdisciplinary activity – characterized by the constant preoccupation of co-
relating disciplines, dealing with and trying to understand the complex and interesting
worlds of the physical, biological, imaginary, and human, all of which are multivariate in
character. ‘The Fundamentals of Design 1’ course is an attempt to sensitize you to this
complex, dynamic and multidimensional scenario – to expand the focus of your discipline,
and place you within the larger context and discourse of design. The main objective of this

## Page 186

Course Abstract: PC 722 FOD 1 for MDes CD

2

module is to acquaint students with the means and methods of approaching, investigating
and solving problems creatively by providing them with appropriate design and conceptual
skill sets – one such primary skill being that of ‘Drawing and Observation’ and ‘Composition’.

Drawing and observation are fundamental to design because they are the primary tools for
understanding and translating the world around us. They help designers move from abstract
ideas to concrete, viable solutions. Observation is the bedrock of the design process. It's
about more than just looking; it's about seeing, and it’s about a mindful, purposeful study of
people, objects, and environments. By observing users in their natural habitats, designers
can gain empathy and uncover unspoken needs, pain points, and behaviors that people may
not even realize they have. This direct insight is crucial for creating designs that are truly
user-centred. Observation also helps designers to gather data, identify opportunities by
noticing what is and what is not working and find inspiration through observing nature, art,
and daily life that can spark new ideas and creative connections between disparate
concepts.

Following on from observation, Drawing is the language of design. It's not about creating a
perfect piece of art, but about a practical tool for thinking and communicating. Drawing
gives shape to abstract ideas, allowing designers to quickly externalize and refine their
thoughts. As a critical skill drawing helps in visual thinking and work through problems,
explore multiple solutions, and develop ideas rapidly. It's far faster and more flexible than
using digital tools in the early stages of a project.

The act of drawing forces you to look at a subject more closely, paying attention to details
like proportion, form, light, and texture. This trains your eye to see the world with a
designer's perspective. By adding a layer of critical and reflexive thinking, drawing becomes
a tool for communication – a rapid way to visually communicate the idea, something that is
visual and tangible, which otherwise would be challenging to use words to communicate.

## Page 187

Course Abstract: PC 722 FOD 1 for MDes CD

3

While Part A: Drawing and observation trains the eye to see and the hand to represent, Part
B: Composition introduces the critical process of strategically arranging of elements to
create a cohesive, clear, and impactful message. A good composition helps one control how
a user's eye moves through a design, ensuring they see the most important information
first. Without it, a design can feel chaotic, cluttered, and confusing, making it difficult for
users to understand its purpose. Composition relies on several key cognitive principles (for
example the Gestalt Theory) that work together to create an effective design. Composition
looks at visual hierarchy which is the order in which a viewer's eye is drawn to elements.
One can create a visual hierarchy by adjusting size, colour, contrast, and position to make
some elements stand out more than others. Using the broad concepts if visual balance,
contrast, rhythm and harmony and integrating with concepts of proximity and repetition the
design can be perceived as a structured group. By grouping related items, one makes
information easier to scan and understand. By using consistent elements like a specific
colour palette, typography, or spacing, the design creates a sense of unity and reinforces a
communication and identity, making the design feel cohesive and professional. By
integrated negative space (or White Space) it gives the user's eye a place to rest, prevents
the design from feeling cluttered, and can be used to emphasize a focal point by isolating it
from other elements.

For any design, whether digital interfaces or analog products, composition directly impacts
user experience. It determines whether a user can quickly find what they're looking for and
accomplish a task. A good composition increases comprehension with a well-composed
design reducing cognitive load. The visual hierarchy guides them, making the information
easy to process. It also improves usability by can creating intuitive layouts. This leads to
higher conversion rates, lower bounce rates, and a more pleasant experience for the user.

Beyond function, a good composition is simply more visually appealing. It creates a sense of
harmony and professionalism that builds trust and makes the user want to engage with the
design.

## Page 188

Course Abstract: PC 722 FOD 1 for MDes CD

4

This module emphasizes the latitude and value of individual thinking and one’s ability to
observe and see, which would enable you to apply yourself creatively in solving problems.
Composition in design terms is defined as structured, thoughtful, compatible placement or
arrangements of elements in space (2-dimension/3-dimension/4-dimension) and/or time.
Composition allows us to incorporate varied elements, combining them into reasonable and
working whole – an example that can be referred to here can be that of cooking – by
combining varied elements in a particular manner and in particular proportions it produces a
palatable preparation. Good composition conveys information, tells a story, draws attention
to the important and useful bits, segregates data, brings in elements of aesthetics and leaves
an indelible imprint in the mind’s eye.

This is a critical foundational course in design.

Course Outcomes:
Knowledge:
1. Observation of objects, nature, indoor, outdoor
2. Observation and imagination
3. Hand, eye, perception
4. Representation, Perspective & Proportion
5. 1-point, 2-point & 3-point perspective
6. Analytical and isometric drawings
7. Human sketching and capturing motion
8. Distilling to capture the essence
9. Composition and its rules
10. Principles of Composition: Balance, Contrast, Harmony, Rhythm, Repetition,
Proximity, Negative Space, Figure-ground
11. Gestalt Theory and its application
12. Application of Gestalt Laws
13. Nature of 2D and 3D spaces
14. Composition in the real world

Skill:

3. Quality, precision and professionalism

5. Judgement
6. Communication
7. Reflexive thinking

Assessment Method: Continuous assessment of application of concepts through the
duration of the course, along with assessment of group and individual assignments given.
Assessment will not only cover the assignments, but professionalism, precision, pride, care
and discipline displayed by the students.

Course Abstract: PC 732 FOD 2 for MDes CD

1
COURSE FILE

PC732: Fundamentals of Design - II

Credit Structure: 3-0-4-5
Program: MDes (Communication Design)
Year: 1
Semester: 1
Duration: 18 Days (Full Day – Lecture + Studio)
Timing: 9 AM – 6 PM

Course Instructor: Anirban Dutta Gupta + Visiting Faculty

Couse Placement: Core course for MDes (CD) Semester 1. This course is composed of two
parts – part A: Typography and Part B: Colour. Part A and Part B is taken consecutively. This is
a foundational course in design.

Course Format: Module based. The course is taught as a combination of Lectures and Studio
sessions. Lectures introduce concepts, which are then analysed and explored via studio
sessions and assignments. Lecture and studio is integrated and in certain cases can overlap.
Teaching is through hand-on learning and through learning-by-doing and via real-time
demonstrations and guidance by faculty members.

Course Objective and Content:
This module deals with the advanced aspects of colour, composition, virtual 3D spaces,
Typography as image, Illustration for information design, as means of explore, visualize and
communicate complex ideas with high levels of data density.

## Page 194

Course Abstract: PC 732 FOD 2 for MDes CD

2
This module would present an entry point to moving image and sound analysis, structure,
methodology, concepts and experimentation for communicating specific ideas as well as to
explore the possibilities and potential of each medium. Consideration of ‘mediums’ would
focus on moving image scenarios that would include cinema, animation, multimedia and
Sound Design and for 2D and virtual 3D environments.

Students will also engage with ‘Design History’ as a means of understanding visual language
that is applied for range of visualization, essential for practice of sketching, photography, film
and animation.
Typography is the art and technique of arranging type to make written language legible,
readable, and visually appealing. It involves selecting typefaces (fonts), adjusting the spacing
between letters (kerning), words (tracking), and lines (leading), and overall creating a visual
style that complements the content and enhances the message being conveyed.
Key elements of typography include Typeface which is the design of the letters with each
shape communicating a distinctive emotion, which evolves into a font which are the
variations within the family for example, Helvetica Bold or Times New Roman Italic. Each
type and its categories are steeped in history as it is usage and the message it
communicates. For example, the gothic Germanic type is associated with the fascist
ideology, hence its usage today may lead to negative interpretation with the values
associated with fascism.
Beyond the selection of type, the second level is to manipulate the font to match to its
usage. This includes understanding kerning - the space between two specific letters to
improve legibility or visual balance, tracking - the space between all letters in a word or
sentence, leading - the vertical space between lines of text, hierarchy - the arrangement of
type elements to show their relative importance, often using different font sizes, weights, or
styles to guide the reader, alignment – whether to position it left, right, center, or justified
and contrast which is the differences in weight, size, or colour to create emphasis and
improve readability.

## Page 195

Course Abstract: PC 732 FOD 2 for MDes CD

3
Good typography can significantly affect how a message is perceived and can even evoke
different emotional responses from the audience. It enhances readability and legibility
ensuring that the text is easy to read. It helps in establishing a Visual Hierarchy by organizing
information by signalling what’s most important. This overlaps with some of the concepts
that were discussed in FOD 1 – Composition module.
Typography also conveys tone and emotion, for example, Serif fonts (like Times New Roman)
tend to be seen as formal, classic, and reliable. Sans-serif fonts (like Helvetica) are more
modern, clean, and minimal. This leads into type as an element of brand design be creating
a distinctive identity (Coca-Cola for example). It also helps in the aesthetic appeal with the
right combination of font choices, spacing, and layout can make a design feel polished,
cohesive, and visually striking, which grabs attention and makes an impact.

In the digital domain, typography is essential for a smooth user experience. If the type is
hard to read, poorly spaced, or mismatched, users will likely get frustrated and leave.
Consistent and accessible typography helps users navigate the content effortlessly.

Overall it guides the message by shaping how the message is understood and sets the mood
for the audience. The wrong choice of typography could make a brand appear
unprofessional, outdated, or inconsistent with its message. Typography, therefore, is a
critical component of visual design because it goes beyond simply choosing a font. It's the
art and technique of arranging type to make written language legible, readable, and
appealing when displayed. This part of the module aims to sensitise the students to the
variations of types and its uses.

The objective of part B i.e. Basics of colour is to sensitise the students to the ‘idea’ of colour
and understand that there is no ‘one’ colour. Colour is tainted by light, culture, our
perception, biology, emotion, chemistry and physics. Within this maze, we will try and
identify and understand some of the broad rules that will allow us to make sense of colour.
The objective is to teach and familiarize students with different methods of determining

## Page 196

Course Abstract: PC 732 FOD 2 for MDes CD

4
successful colour schemes that will enhance their designs, as well as the emotional
effectiveness that those choices can have in the marketplace. As part of this course, we will
understand colour theory and derivations of colour. It will also acquaint you to shades, tints
and tones as well as colour harmony and colour interaction. This overall understanding of
colour will be an essential stepping stone to take conscious decisions on application of
colour.

Colour is an integral component of design, playing a crucial role in shaping the visual and
emotional experience of an audience. Colour is one of the most immediate and influential
elements in design. The strategic use of colour can transform a design from a mere visual
arrangement to a meaningful experience. The psychological effects of colour have long been
studied in fields such as marketing, psychology, and design. Different colours evoke distinct
emotional responses, which can influence perceptions and behaviour. For example, warm
colours are commonly associated with energy, warmth, and urgency, while cool colours like
often evoke calmness, trust, and professionalism. Understanding these emotional
associations allows designers to select colours that align with the desired mood and tone of
the design. It therefore, plays a fundamental role in establishing and reinforcing brand
identity. The selection of a particular colour palette is often a deliberate decision aimed at
differentiating a brand from its competitors while conveying specific values and messages.
Consistent use of colour across various media platforms enhances brand recognition and
fosters a sense of familiarity and trust with the audience.

Beyond its aesthetic and emotional impact, colour is also critical to the functionality and
accessibility of a design. In digital design, for example, the contrast between text and
background colours directly affects readability. A lack of sufficient contrast can make
content difficult to read, leading to a poor user experience. Therefore, colour choices must
be made with consideration of legibility and ease of navigation.

## Page 197

Course Abstract: PC 732 FOD 2 for MDes CD

5
Colours also carry specific cultural meanings that vary across different regions and societies.
The students are encouraged to link the use and significance of colour to the course in
Culture and Communications. For instance, in Western cultures, white is often associated
with purity and peace, whereas in some Eastern cultures, it symbolizes mourning and death.
Designers must be aware of these cultural connotations to avoid miscommunication or
unintended negative associations when designing for global audiences. Moreover, colour
symbolism can be employed strategically to reinforce the intended message of a design.

Colour is fundamental to the creation of aesthetic cohesion and visual harmony within a
design. A well-thought-out colour palette can unify disparate elements, creating a balanced
and pleasing composition. The principles of colour theory, including complementary,
analogous, and triadic colour schemes, guide designers in selecting harmonious
combinations that are visually engaging and balanced. Colour adds another dimension to
the principles of composition that was explored in FOD 1.

Knowledge and Skill targets:

2. History of Colour
3. Colour and emotion
4. Colour interaction
5. Colour and culture
6. Colour and Design
7. Typography and its component
8. History of typography
9. Type and emotion
10. Type and design
